LG1871	1	ONE'S-SELF I sing—a simple, separate Person;
LG1871	2	Yet utter the word Democratic, the word En-masse.
LG1871	3	Of Physiology from top to toe I sing;
LG1871	4	Not physiognomy alone, nor brain alone, is worthy for the muse—I say the Form complete is worthier far;
LG1871	5	The Female equally with the male I sing.
LG1871	6	Of Life immense in passion, pulse, and power,
LG1871	7	Cheerful—for freest action form'd, under the laws di-vinedivine,
LG1871	8	The Modern Man I sing.
LG1871	9	AS I ponder'd in silence,
LG1871	10	Returning upon my poems, considering, lingering long,
LG1871	11	A Phantom arose before me, with distrustful aspect,
LG1871	12	Terrible in beauty, age, and power,
LG1871	13	The genius of poets of old lands,
LG1871	14	As to me directing like flame its eyes,
LG1871	15	With finger pointing to many immortal songs,
LG1871	16	And menacing voice, What singest thou? it said;
LG1871	17	Knowest thou not, there is but one theme for ever-enduring bards?
LG1871	18	And that is the theme of War, the fortune of battles,
LG1871	19	The making of perfect soldiers?
LG1871	20	Be it so, then I answer'd,
LG1871	21	I too, haughty Shade, also sing war—and a longer and greater one than any,
LG1871	22	Waged in my book with varying fortune—with fight, ad-vanceadvance, and retreat—Victory deferr'd and wavering,
LG1871	23	(Yet, methinks, certain, or as good as certain, at the last,) —The field the world;
LG1871	24	For life and death—for the Body, and for the eternal Soul,
LG1871	25	Lo! I too am come, chanting the chant of battles,
LG1871	26	I, above all, promote brave soldiers.
LG1871	27	IN cabin'd ships, at sea,
LG1871	28	The boundless blue on every side expanding,
LG1871	29	With whistling winds and music of the waves—the large imperious waves—In such,
LG1871	30	Or some lone bark, buoy'd on the dense marine,
LG1871	31	Where, joyous, full of faith, spreading white sails,
LG1871	32	She cleaves the ether, mid the sparkle and the foam of day, or under many a star at night,
LG1871	33	By sailors young and old, haply will I, a reminiscence of the land, be read,
LG1871	34	In full rapport at last.
LG1871	35	Here are our thoughts—voyagers' thoughts,
LG1871	36	Here not the land, firm land, alone appears, may then by them be said;
LG1871	37	The sky o'erarches here—we feel the undulating deck be-neathbeneath our feet,
LG1871	38	We feel the long pulsation—ebb and flow of endless mo-tion;motion
LG1871	39	The tones of unseen mystery—the vague and vast sugges-tionssuggestions of the briny world—the liquid-flowing sylla-blessyllables,
LG1871	40	The perfume, the faint creaking of the cordage, the melan-cholymelancholy rhythm,
LG1871	41	The boundless vista, and the horizon far and dim, are all here,
LG1871	42	And this is Ocean's poem.
LG1871	43	Then falter not, O book! fulfil your destiny!
LG1871	44	You, not a reminiscence of the land alone,
LG1871	45	You too, as a lone bark, cleaving the ether—purpos'd I know not whither—yet ever full of faith,
LG1871	46	Consort to every ship that sails—sail you!
LG1871	47	Bear forth to them, folded, my love —(Dear mariners! for you I fold it here, in every leaf;)
LG1871	48	Speed on, my Book! spread your white sails, my little bark, athwart the imperious waves!
LG1871	49	Chant on—sail on—bear o'er the boundless blue, from me, to every shore,
LG1871	50	This song for mariners and all their ships.
LG1871	51	I HEARD that you ask'd for something to prove this puzzle, the New World,
LG1871	52	And to define America, her athletic Democracy;
LG1871	53	Therefore I send you my poems, that you behold in them what you wanted.
LG1871	54	YOU who celebrate bygones!
LG1871	55	Who have explored the outward, the surfaces of the races—the life that has exhibited itself;
LG1871	56	Who have treated of man as the creature of politics, aggregates, rulers and priests;
LG1871	57	I, habitan of the Alleghanies, treating of him as he is in himself, in his own rights,
LG1871	58	Pressing the pulse of the life that has seldom exhibited itself, (the great pride of man in himself;)
LG1871	59	Chanter of Personality, outlining what is yet to be,
LG1871	60	I project the history of the future.
LG1871	61	FOR him I sing,
LG1871	62	I raise the Present on the Past,
LG1871	63	(As some perennial tree, out of its roots, the present on the past:)
LG1871	64	With time and space I him dilate—and fuse the im-mortalimmortal laws,
LG1871	65	To make himself, by them, the law unto himself.
LG1871	66	WHEN I read the book, the biography famous,
LG1871	67	And is this, then, (said I,) what the author calls a man's life?
LG1871	68	And so will some one, when I am dead and gone, write my life?
LG1871	69	(As if any man really knew aught of my life;
LG1871	70	Why, even I myself, I often think, know little or noth-ingnothing of my real life;
LG1871	71	Only a few hints—a few diffused, faint clues and indi-rectionsindirections,
LG1871	72	I seek, for my own use, to trace out here.)
LG1871	73	BEGINNING my studies, the first step pleas'd me so much,
LG1871	74	The mere fact, consciousness—these forms—the power of motion,
LG1871	75	The least insect or animal—the senses—eyesight— love;
LG1871	76	The first step, I say, aw'd me and pleas'd me so much,
LG1871	77	I have hardly gone, and hardly wish'd to go, any far-therfarther,
LG1871	78	But stop and loiter all the time, to sing it in extatic songs.
LG1871	79	To thee, old Cause!
LG1871	80	Thou peerless, passionate, good cause!
LG1871	81	Thou stern, remorseless, sweet Idea!
LG1871	82	Deathless throughout the ages, races, lands!
LG1871	83	After a strange, sad war—great war for thee,
LG1871	84	(I think all war through time was really fought, and ever will be really fought, for thee;)
LG1871	85	These chants for thee—the eternal march of thee.
LG1871	86	Thou orb of many orbs!
LG1871	87	Thou seething principle! Thou well-kept, latent germ!
LG1871	88	Thou centre!
LG1871	89	Around the idea of thee the strange sad war revolv-ingrevolving,
LG1871	90	With all its angry and vehement play of causes,
LG1871	91	(With yet unknown results to come, for thrice a thou-sandthousand years,)
LG1871	92	These recitatives for thee—my Book and the War are one,
LG1871	93	Merged in its spirit I and mine—as the contest hinged on thee,
LG1871	94	As a wheel on its axis turns, this Book, unwitting to itself,
LG1871	95	Around the Idea of thee.
LG1871	96	STARTING from fish-shape Paumanok, where I was born,
LG1871	97	Well-begotten, and rais'd by a perfect mother;
LG1871	98	After roaming many lands—lover of populous pave-mentspavements;
LG1871	99	Dweller in Mannahatta, my city—or on southern sa-vannassavannas;
LG1871	100	Or a soldier camp'd, or carrying my knapsack and gun— or a miner in California;
LG1871	101	Or rude in my home in Dakota's woods, my diet meat, my drink from the spring;
LG1871	102	Or withdrawn to muse and meditate in some deep re-cessrecess,
LG1871	103	Far from the clank of crowds, intervals passing, rapt and happy;
LG1871	104	Aware of the fresh free giver, the flowing Missouri— aware of mighty Niagara;
LG1871	105	Aware of the buffalo herds, grazing the plains—the hirsute and strong-breasted bull;
LG1871	106	Of earth, rocks, Fifth-month flowers, experienced— stars, rain, snow, my amaze;
LG1871	107	Having studied the mocking-bird's tones, and the mountain-hawk's,
LG1871	108	And heard at dusk the unrival'd one, the hermit thrush from the swamp-cedars,
LG1871	109	Solitary, singing in the West, I strike up for a New World.
LG1871	110	Victory, union, faith, identity, time,
LG1871	111	The indissoluble compacts, riches, mystery,
LG1871	112	Eternal progress, the kosmos, and the modern reports.
LG1871	113	This, then, is life;
LG1871	114	Here is what has come to the surface after so many throes and convulsions.
LG1871	115	How curious! how real!
LG1871	116	Underfoot the divine soil—overhead the sun.
LG1871	117	See, revolving, the globe;
LG1871	118	The ancestor-continents, away, group'd together;
LG1871	119	The present and future continents, north and south, with the isthmus between.
LG1871	120	See, vast, trackless spaces;
LG1871	121	As in a dream, they change, they swiftly fill;
LG1871	122	Countless masses debouch upon them;
LG1871	123	They are now cover'd with the foremost people, arts, institutions, known.
LG1871	124	See, projected, through time,
LG1871	125	For me, an audience interminable.
LG1871	126	With firm and regular step they wend—they never stop,
LG1871	127	Successions of men, Americanos, a hundred millions;
LG1871	128	One generation playing its part, and passing on;
LG1871	129	Another generation playing its part, and passing on in its turn,
LG1871	130	With faces turn'd sideways or backward towards me, to listen,
LG1871	131	With eyes retrospective towards me.
LG1871	132	Americanos! conquerors! marches humanitarian;
LG1871	133	Foremost! century marches! Libertad! masses!
LG1871	134	For you a programme of chants.
LG1871	135	Chants of the prairies;
LG1871	136	Chants of the long-running Mississippi, and down to the Mexican sea;
LG1871	137	Chants of Ohio, Indiana, Illinois, Iowa, Wisconsin and Minnesota;
LG1871	138	Chants going forth from the centre, from Kansas, and thence, equi-distant,
LG1871	139	Shooting in pulses of fire, ceaseless, to vivify all.
LG1871	140	In the Year 80 of The States,
LG1871	141	My tongue, every atom of my blood, form'd from this soil, this air,
LG1871	142	Born here of parents born here, from parents the same, and their parents the same,
LG1871	143	I, now thirty-six years old, in perfect health, begin,
LG1871	144	Hoping to cease not till death.
LG1871	145	Creeds and schools in abeyance,
LG1871	146	(Retiring back a while, sufficed at what they are, but never forgotten,)
LG1871	147	I harbor, for good or bad—I permit to speak, at every hazard,
LG1871	148	Nature now without check, with original energy.
LG1871	149	Take my leaves, America! take them, South, and take them, North!
LG1871	150	Make welcome for them everywhere, for they are your own offspring;
LG1871	151	Surround them, East and West! for they would sur-roundsurround you;
LG1871	152	And you precedents! connect lovingly with them, for they connect lovingly with you.
LG1871	153	I conn'd old times:
LG1871	154	I sat studying at the feet of the great masters:
LG1871	155	Now, if eligible, O that the great masters might return and study me!
LG1871	156	In the name of These States, shall I scorn the an-tiqueantique?
LG1871	157	Why These are the children of the antique, to jus-tifyjustify it.
LG1871	158	Dead poets, philosophs, priests,
LG1871	159	Martyrs, artists, inventors, governments long since,
LG1871	160	Language-shapers, on other shores,
LG1871	161	Nations once powerful, now reduced, withdrawn, or desolate,
LG1871	162	I dare not proceed till I respectfully credit what you have left, wafted hither:
LG1871	163	I have perused it—own it is admirable, (moving awhile among it;)
LG1871	164	Think nothing can ever be greater—nothing can ever deserve more than it deserves;
LG1871	165	Regarding it all intently a long while—then dismiss-ingdismissing it,
LG1871	166	I stand in my place, with my own day, here.
LG1871	167	Here lands female and male;
LG1871	168	Here the heir-ship and heiress-ship of the world—here the flame of materials;
LG1871	169	Here Spirituality, the translatress, the openly-avow'd,
LG1871	170	The ever-tending, the finale of visible forms;
LG1871	171	The satisfier, after due long-waiting, now advancing,
LG1871	172	Yes, here comes my mistress, the Soul.
LG1871	173	The SOUL:
LG1871	174	Forever and forever—longer than soil is brown and solid—longer than water ebbs and flows.
LG1871	175	I will make the poems of materials, for I think they are to be the most spiritual poems;
LG1871	176	And I will make the poems of my body and of mor-talitymortality,
LG1871	177	For I think I shall then supply myself with the poems of my Soul, and of immortality.
LG1871	178	I will make a song for These States, that no one State may under any circumstances be subjected to another State;
LG1871	179	And I will make a song that there shall be comity by day and by night between all The States, and between any two of them;
LG1871	180	And I will make a song for the ears of the President, full of weapons with menacing points,
LG1871	181	And behind the weapons countless dissatisfied faces:
LG1871	182	—And a song make I, of the One form'd out of all;
LG1871	183	The fang'd and glittering One whose head is over all;
LG1871	184	Resolute, warlike One, including and over all;
LG1871	185	(However high the head of any else, that head is over all.)
LG1871	186	I will acknowledge contemporary lands;
LG1871	187	I will trail the whole geography of the globe, and sa-lutesalute courteously every city large and small;
LG1871	188	And employments! I will put in my poems, that with you is heroism, upon land and sea;
LG1871	189	And I will report all heroism from an American point of view.
LG1871	190	I will sing the song of companionship;
LG1871	191	I will show what alone must finally compact These;
LG1871	192	I believe These are to found their own ideal of manly love, indicating it in me;
LG1871	193	I will therefore let flame from me the burning fires that were threatening to consume me;
LG1871	194	I will lift what has too long kept down those smoulder-ingsmouldering fires;
LG1871	195	I will give them complete abandonment;
LG1871	196	I will write the evangel-poem of comrades, and of love;
LG1871	197	(For who but I should understand love, with all its sor-rowsorrow and joy?
LG1871	198	And who but I should be the poet of comrades?)
LG1871	199	I am the credulous man of qualities, ages, races;
LG1871	200	I advance from the people in their own spirit;
LG1871	201	Here is what sings unrestricted faith.
LG1871	202	Omnes! Omnes! let others ignore what they may;
LG1871	203	I make the poem of evil also—I commemorate that part also;
LG1871	204	I am myself just as much evil as good, and my nation is—And I say there is in fact no evil;
LG1871	205	(Or if there is, I say it is just as important to you, to the land, or to me, as anything else.)
LG1871	206	I too, following many, and follow'd by many, inau-gurateinaugurate a Religion—I descend into the arena;
LG1871	207	(It may be I am destin'd to utter the loudest cries there, the winner's pealing shouts;
LG1871	208	Who knows? they may rise from me yet, and soar above every thing.)
LG1871	209	Each is not for its own sake;
LG1871	210	I say the whole earth, and all the stars in the sky, are for Religion's sake.
LG1871	211	I say no man has ever yet been half devout enough;
LG1871	212	None has ever yet adored or worship'd half enough;
LG1871	213	None has begun to think how divine he himself is, and how certain the future is.
LG1871	214	I say that the real and permanent grandeur of These States must be their Religion;
LG1871	215	Otherwise there is no real and permanent grandeur:
LG1871	216	(Nor character, nor life worthy the name, without Reli-gionReligion;
LG1871	217	Nor land, nor man or woman, without Religion.)
LG1871	218	What are you doing, young man?
LG1871	219	Are you so earnest—so given up to literature, science, art, amours?
LG1871	220	These ostensible realities, politics, points?
LG1871	221	Your ambition or business, whatever it may be?
LG1871	222	It is well—Against such I say not a word—I am their poet also;
LG1871	223	But behold! such swiftly subside— burnt up for Reli-gion'sReligion's sake;
LG1871	224	For not all matter is fuel to heat, impalpable flame, the essential life of the earth,
LG1871	225	Any more than such are to Religion.
LG1871	226	What do you seek, so pensive and silent?
LG1871	227	What do you need, Camerado?
LG1871	228	Dear son! do you think it is love?
LG1871	229	Listen, dear son—listen, America, daughter or son!
LG1871	230	It is a painful thing to love a man or woman to excess —and yet it satisfies—it is great;
LG1871	231	But there is something else very great—it makes the whole coincide;
LG1871	232	It, magnificent, beyond materials, with continuous hands, sweeps and provides for all.
LG1871	233	Know you! solely to drop in the earth the germs of a greater Religion,
LG1871	234	The following chants, each for its kind, I sing.
LG1871	235	My comrade!
LG1871	236	For you, to share with me, two greatnesses—and a third one, rising inclusive and more resplendent,
LG1871	237	The greatness of Love and Democracy—and the great-nessgreatness of Religion.
LG1871	238	Melange mine own! the unseen and the seen;
LG1871	239	Mysterious ocean where the streams empty;
LG1871	240	Prophetic spirit of materials shifting and flickering around me;
LG1871	241	Living beings, identities, now doubtless near us, in the air, that we know not of;
LG1871	242	Contact daily and hourly that will not release me;
LG1871	243	These selecting—these, in hints, demanded of me.
LG1871	244	Not he, with a daily kiss, onward from childhood kissing me,
LG1871	245	Has winded and twisted around me that which holds me to him,
LG1871	246	Any more than I am held to the heavens, to the spir-itualspiritual world,
LG1871	247	And to the identities of the Gods, my lovers, faithful and true,
LG1871	248	After what they have done to me, suggesting themes.
LG1871	249	O such themes! Equalities!
LG1871	250	O amazement of things! O divine average!
LG1871	251	O warblings under the sun—usher'd, as now, at noon, or setting!
LG1871	252	O strain, musical, flowing through ages—now reaching hither!
LG1871	253	I take to your reckless and composite chords—I add to them, and cheerfully pass them forward.
LG1871	254	As I have walk'd in Alabama my morning walk
LG1871	255	I have seen where the she-bird, the mocking-bird, sat on her nest in the briers, hatching her brood.
LG1871	256	I have seen the he-bird also;
LG1871	257	I have paused to hear him, near at hand, inflating his throat, and joyfully singing.
LG1871	258	And while I paused, it came to me that what he really sang for was not there only,
LG1871	259	Nor for his mate, nor himself only, nor all sent back by the echoes;
LG1871	260	But subtle, clandestine, away beyond,
LG1871	261	A charge transmitted, and gift occult, for those being born.
LG1871	262	Democracy!
LG1871	263	Near at hand to you a throat is now inflating itself and joyfully singing.
LG1871	264	Ma femme!
LG1871	265	For the brood beyond us and of us,
LG1871	266	For those who belong here, and those to come,
LG1871	267	I, exultant, to be ready for them, will now shake out carols stronger and haughtier than have ever yet been heard upon earth.
LG1871	268	I will make the songs of passion, to give them their way,
LG1871	269	And your songs, outlaw'd offenders—for I scan you with kindred eyes, and carry you with me the same as any.
LG1871	270	I will make the true poem of riches,
LG1871	271	To earn for the body and the mind whatever adheres, and goes forward, and is not dropt by death.
LG1871	272	I will effuse egotism, and show it underlying all—and I will be the bard of personality;
LG1871	273	And I will show of male and female that either is but the equal of the other;
LG1871	274	And sexual organs and acts! do you concentrate in me —for I am determin'd to tell you with courageous clear voice, to prove you illustrious;
LG1871	275	And I will show that there is no imperfection in the present—and can be none in the future;
LG1871	276	And I will show that whatever happens to anybody, it may be turn'd to beautiful results—and I will show that nothing can happen more beautiful than death;
LG1871	277	And I will thread a thread through my poems that time and events are compact,
LG1871	278	And that all the things of the universe are perfect mira-clesmiracles, each as profound as any.
LG1871	279	I will not make poems with reference to parts;
LG1871	280	But I will make leaves, poems, poemets, songs, says, thoughts, with reference to ensemble:
LG1871	281	And I will not sing with reference to a day, but with reference to all days;
LG1871	282	And I will not make a poem, nor the least part of a poem, but has reference to the Soul;
LG1871	283	(Because, having look'd at the objects of the universe, I find there is no one, nor any particle of one, but has reference to the Soul.)
LG1871	284	Was somebody asking to see the Soul?
LG1871	285	See! your own shape and countenance—persons, sub-stancessubstances, beasts, the trees, the running rivers, the rocks and sands.
LG1871	286	All hold spiritual joys, and afterwards loosen them:
LG1871	287	How can the real body ever die, and be buried?
LG1871	288	Of your real body, and any man's or woman's real body,
LG1871	289	Item for item, it will elude the hands of the corpse-cleanerscorpse-cleaners, and pass to fitting spheres,
LG1871	290	Carrying what has accrued to it from the moment of birth to the moment of death.
LG1871	291	Not the types set up by the printer return their im-pressionimpression, the meaning, the main concern,
LG1871	292	Any more than a man's substance and life, or a wo-man'swoman's substance and life, return in the body and the Soul,
LG1871	293	Indifferently before death and after. death.
LG1871	294	Behold! the body includes and is the meaning, the main concern—and includes and is the Soul;
LG1871	295	Whoever you are! how superb and how divine is your body, or any part of it.
LG1871	296	Whoever you are! to you endless announcements.
LG1871	297	Daughter of the lands, did you wait for your poet?
LG1871	298	Did you wait for one with a flowing mouth and indica-tiveindicative hand?
LG1871	299	Toward the male of The States, and toward the fe-malefemale of The States,
LG1871	300	Live words—words to the lands.
LG1871	301	O the lands! interlink'd, food-yielding lands!
LG1871	302	Land of coal and iron! Land of gold! Lands of cot-toncotton, sugar, rice!
LG1871	303	Land of wheat, beef, pork! Land of wool and hemp! Land of the apple and grape!
LG1871	304	Land of the pastoral plains, the grass-fields of the world! Land of those sweet-air'd interminable plateaus!
LG1871	305	Land of the herd, the garden, the healthy house of adobie!
LG1871	306	Lands where the north-west Columbia winds, and where the south-west Colorado winds!
LG1871	307	Land of the eastern Chesapeake! Land of the Dela-wareDelaware!
LG1871	308	Land of Ontario, Erie, Huron, Michigan!
LG1871	309	Land of the Old Thirteen! Massachusetts land! Land of Vermont and Connecticut!
LG1871	310	Land of the ocean shores! Land of sierras and peaks!
LG1871	311	Land of boatmen and sailors! Fishermen's land!
LG1871	312	Inextricable lands! the clutch'd together! the passion-atepassionate ones!
LG1871	313	The side by side! the elder and younger brothers! the bony-limb'd!
LG1871	314	The great women's land! the feminine! the experienced sisters and the inexperienced sisters!
LG1871	315	Far breath'd land! Arctic braced! Mexican breez'd! the diverse! the compact!
LG1871	316	The Pennsylvanian! the Virginian! the double Caro-linianCarolinian!
LG1871	317	O all and each well-loved by me! my intrepid nations! O I at any rate include you all with perfect love!
LG1871	318	I cannot be discharged from you! not from one, any sooner than another!
LG1871	319	O Death! O for all that, I am yet of you, unseen, this hour, with irrepressible love,
LG1871	320	Walking New England, a friend, a traveler,
LG1871	321	Splashing my bare feet in the edge of the summer rip-plesripples, on Paumanok's sands,
LG1871	322	Crossing the praries—dwelling again in Chicago—dwel-lingdwelling in every town,
LG1871	323	Observing shows, births, improvements, structures, arts,
LG1871	324	Listening to the orators and the oratresses in public halls,
LG1871	325	Of and through The States, as during life—each man and woman my neighbor,
LG1871	326	The Louisianian, the Georgian, as near to me, and I as near to him and her,
LG1871	327	The Mississippian and Arkansian yet with me—and I yet with any of them;
LG1871	328	Yet upon the plains west of the spinal river—yet in my house of adobie,
LG1871	329	Yet returning eastward—yet in the Sea-Side State, or in Maryland,
LG1871	330	Yet Kanadian, cheerily braving the winter—the snow and ice welcome to me,
LG1871	331	Yet a true son either of Maine, or of the Granite State, or of the Narragansett Bay State, or of the Empire State;
LG1871	332	Yet sailing to other shores to annex the same—yet welcoming every new brother;
LG1871	333	Hereby applying these leaves to the new ones, from the hour they unite with the old ones;
LG1871	334	Coming among the new ones myself, to be their com-panioncompanion and equal—coming personally to you now;
LG1871	335	Enjoining you to acts, characters, spectacles, with me.
LG1871	336	With me, with firm holding—yet haste, haste on.
LG1871	337	For your life adhere to me!
LG1871	338	Of all the men of the earth, I only can unloose you and toughen you;
LG1871	339	I may have to be persuaded many times before I con-sentconsent to give myself really to you—but what of that?
LG1871	340	Must not Nature be persuaded many times?
LG1871	341	No dainty dolce affettuoso I;
LG1871	342	Bearded, sun-burnt, gray-neck'd, forbidding, I have arrived,
LG1871	343	To be wrestled with as I pass, for the solid prizes of the universe;
LG1871	344	For such I afford whoever can persevere to win them.
LG1871	345	On my way a moment I pause;
LG1871	346	Here for you! and here for America!
LG1871	347	Still the Present I raise aloft—Still the Future of The States I harbinge, glad and sublime;
LG1871	348	And for the Past, I pronounce what the air holds of the red aborigines.
LG1871	349	The red aborigines!
LG1871	350	Leaving natural breaths, sounds of rain and winds, calls as of birds and animals in the woods, syllabled to us for names;
LG1871	351	Okonee, Koosa, Ottawa, Monongahela, Sauk, Natchez, Chattahoochee, Kaqueta, Oronoco,
LG1871	352	Wabash, Miami, Saginaw, Chippewa, Oshkosh, Walla-WallaWalla-Walla;
LG1871	353	Leaving such to The States, they melt, they depart, charging the water and the land with names.
LG1871	354	O expanding and swift! O henceforth,
LG1871	355	Elements, breeds, adjustments, turbulent, quick, and audacious;
LG1871	356	A world primal again—Vistas of glory, incessant and branching;
LG1871	357	A new race, dominating previous ones, and grander far—with new contests,
LG1871	358	New politics, new literatures and religions, new in-ventionsinventions and arts.
LG1871	359	These! my voice announcing—I will sleep no more, but arise;
LG1871	360	You oceans that have been calm within me! how I feel you, fathomless, stirring, preparing unpre-cendentedunprecedented waves and storms.
LG1871	361	See! steamers steaming through my poems!
LG1871	362	See, in my poems immigrants continually coming and landing:
LG1871	363	See, in arriere, the wigwam, the trail, the hunter's hut, the flat-boat, the maize-leaf, the claim, the rude fence, and the backwoods village;
LG1871	364	See, on the one side the Western Sea, and on the other the Eastern Sea, how they advance and retreat upon my poems, as upon their own shores.
LG1871	365	See, pastures and forests in my poems—See, animals, wild and tame—See, beyond the Kansas, count-lesscountless herds of buffalo, feeding on short curly grass;
LG1871	366	See, in my poems, cities, solid, vast, inland, with paved streets, with iron and stone edifices, ceaseless vehicles, and commerce;
LG1871	367	See, the many-cylinder'd steam printing-press—See, the electric telegraph, stretching across the Continent, from the Western Sea to Manhat-tanManhattan;
LG1871	368	See, through Atlantica's depths, pulses American, Europe reaching—pulses of Europe, duly re-turn'dreturn'd;
LG1871	369	See, the strong and quick locomotive, as it departs, panting, blowing the steam whistle;
LG1871	370	See, ploughmen, ploughing farms—See, miners, dig-gingdigging mines—See, the numberless factories;
LG1871	371	See, mechanics, busy at their benches, with tools— See, from among them, superior judges, philo-sophsphilosophs, Presidents, emerge, drest in working dresses;
LG1871	372	See, lounging through the shops and fields of The States, me, well-belov'd, close-held by day and night;
LG1871	373	Hear the loud echoes of my songs there! Read the hints come at last.
LG1871	374	O Camerado close!
LG1871	375	O you and me at last—and us two only.
LG1871	376	O a word to clear one's path ahead endlessly!
LG1871	377	O something extatic and undemonstrable! O music wild!
LG1871	378	O now I triumph—and you shall also;
LG1871	379	O hand in hand—O wholesome pleasure—O one more desirer and lover!
LG1871	380	O to haste, firm holding—to haste, haste on, with me.
LG1871	381	Lo! THE unbounded sea!
LG1871	382	On its breast a Ship starting, spreading all her sails— an ample Ship, carrying even her moonsails;
LG1871	383	The pennant is flying aloft, as she speeds, she speeds so stately— below, emulous waves press forward,
LG1871	384	They surround the Ship, with shining curving motions, and foam.
LG1871	385	UNFOLDED out of the folds of the woman, man comes unfolded, and is always to come unfolded;
LG1871	386	Unfolded only out of the superbest woman of the earth, is to come the superbest man of the earth;
LG1871	387	Unfolded out of the friendliest woman, is to come the friendliest man;
LG1871	388	Unfolded only out of the perfect body of a woman, can a man be form'd of perfect body;
LG1871	389	Unfolded only out of the inimitable poem of the wo-manwoman can come the poems of man—(only thence have my poems come;)
LG1871	390	Unfolded out of the strong and arrogant woman I love, only thence can appear the strong and arrogant man I love;
LG1871	391	Unfolded by brawny embraces from the well-muscled woman I love, only thence come the brawny em-bracesembraces of the man;
LG1871	392	Unfolded out of the folds of the woman's brain, come all the folds of the man's brain, duly obedient;
LG1871	393	Unfolded out of the justice of the woman, all justice is unfolded;
LG1871	394	Unfolded out of the sympathy of the woman is all sym-pathysympathy:
LG1871	395	A man is a great thing upon the earth, and through eternity—but every jot of the greatness of man is unfolded out of woman,
LG1871	396	First the man is shaped in the woman, he can then be shaped in himself.
LG1871	397	STRANGER! if you, passing, meet me, and desire to speak to me, why should you not speak to me?
LG1871	398	And why should I not speak to you?
LG1871	399	I CELEBRATE myself;
LG1871	400	And what I assume you shall assume;
LG1871	401	For every atom belonging to me, as good belongs to you.
LG1871	402	I loafe and invite my Soul;
LG1871	403	I lean and loafe at my ease, observing a spear of sum-mersummer grass.
LG1871	404	Houses and rooms are full of perfumes—the shelves are crowded with perfumes;
LG1871	405	I breathe the fragrance myself, and know it and like it;
LG1871	406	The distillation would intoxicate me also, but I shall not let it.
LG1871	407	The atmosphere is not a perfume—it has no taste of
LG1871	408	the distillation—it is odorless;
LG1871	409	It is for my mouth forever—I am in love with it;
LG1871	410	I will go to the bank by the wood, and become undis-guisedundisguised and naked;
LG1871	411	I am mad for it to be in contact with me.
LG1871	412	The smoke of my own breath;
LG1871	413	Echoes, ripples, buzz'd whispers, love-root, silk-thread, crotch and vine;
LG1871	414	My respiration and inspiration, the beating of my heart, the passing of blood and air through my lungs;
LG1871	415	The sniff of green leaves and dry leaves, and of the shore, and dark-color'd sea-rocks, and of hay in the barn;
LG1871	416	The sound of the belch'd words of my voice, words loos'd to the eddies of the wind;
LG1871	417	A few light kisses, a few embraces, a reaching around of arms;
LG1871	418	The play of shine and shade on the trees as the supple boughs wag;
LG1871	419	The delight alone, or in the rush of the streets, or along the fields and hill-sides;
LG1871	420	The feeling of health, the full noon-trill, the song of me rising from bed and meeting the sun.
LG1871	421	Have you reckon'd a thousand acres much? have you reckon'd the earth much?
LG1871	422	Have you practis'd so long to learn to read?
LG1871	423	Have you felt so proud to get at the meaning of poems?
LG1871	424	Stop this day and night with me, and you shall pos-sesspossess the origin of all poems;
LG1871	425	You shall possess the good of the earth and sun—(there are millions of suns left;)
LG1871	426	You shall no longer take things at second or third hand, nor look through the eyes of the dead, nor feed on the spectres in books;
LG1871	427	You shall not look through my eyes either, nor take things from me:
LG1871	428	You shall listen to all sides, and filter them from your-selfyourself.
LG1871	429	I have heard what the talkers were talking, the talk of the beginning and the end;
LG1871	430	But I do not talk of the beginning or the end.
LG1871	431	There was never any more inception than there is now,
LG1871	432	Nor any more youth or age than there is now;
LG1871	433	And will never be any more perfection than there is now,
LG1871	434	Nor any more heaven or hell than there is now.
LG1871	435	Urge, and urge, and urge;
LG1871	436	Always the procreant urge of the world.
LG1871	437	Out of the dimness opposite equals advance—always substance and increase, always sex;
LG1871	438	Always a knit of identity—always distinction—always a breed of life.
LG1871	439	To elaborate is no avail—learn'd and unlearn'd feel that it is so.
LG1871	440	Sure as the most certain sure, plumb in the uprights, well entretied, braced in the beams,
LG1871	441	Stout as a horse, affectionate, haughty, electrical,
LG1871	442	I and this mystery, here we stand.
LG1871	443	Clear and sweet is my Soul, and clear and sweet is all that is not my Soul.
LG1871	444	Lack one lacks both, and the unseen is proved by the seen,
LG1871	445	Till that becomes unseen, and receives proof in its turn.
LG1871	446	Showing the best, and dividing it from the worst age vexes age;
LG1871	447	Knowing the perfect fitness and equanimity of things, while they discuss I am silent, and go bathe and admire myself.
LG1871	448	Welcome is every organ and attribute of me, and of any man hearty and clean;
LG1871	449	Not an inch, nor a particle of an inch, is vile, and none shall be less familiar than the rest.
LG1871	450	I am satisfied—I see, dance, laugh, sing;
LG1871	451	As the hugging and loving Bed-fellow sleeps at my side through the night, and withdraws at the peep of the day, with stealthy tread,
LG1871	452	Leaving me baskets cover'd with white towels, swelling the house with their plenty,
LG1871	453	Shall I postpone my acceptation and realization, and scream at my eyes,
LG1871	454	That they turn from gazing after and down the road,
LG1871	455	And forthwith cipher and show me a cent,
LG1871	456	Exactly the contents of one, and exactly the contents of two, and which is ahead?
LG1871	457	Trippers and askers surround me;
LG1871	458	People I meet—the effect upon me of my early life, or the ward and city I live in, or the nation,
LG1871	459	The latest dates, discoveries, inventions, societies, authors old and new,
LG1871	460	My dinner, dress, associates, looks, compliments, dues,
LG1871	461	The real or fancied indifference of some man or woman I love,
LG1871	462	The sickness of one of my folks, or of myself, or ill-doingill-doing, or loss or lack of money, or depressions or exaltations;
LG1871	463	Battles, the horrors of fratricidal war, the fever of doubtful news, the fitful events;
LG1871	464	These come to me days and nights, and go from me again,
LG1871	465	But they are not the Me myself.
LG1871	466	Apart from the pulling and hauling stands what I am;
LG1871	467	Stands amused, complacent, compassionating, idle, uni-taryunitary;
LG1871	468	Looks down, is erect, or bends an arm on an impalpable certain rest,
LG1871	469	Looking with side-curved head, curious what will come next;
LG1871	470	Both in and out of the game, and watching and won-deringwondering at it.
LG1871	471	Backward I see in my own days where I sweated through fog with linguists and contenders;
LG1871	472	I have no mockings or arguments—I witness and wait.
LG1871	473	I believe in you, my Soul—the other I am must not abase itself to you;
LG1871	474	And you must not be abased to the other.
LG1871	475	Loafe with me on the grass—loose the stop from your throat;
LG1871	476	Not words, not music or rhyme I want—not custom or lecture, not even the best;
LG1871	477	Only the lull I like, the hum of your valved voice.
LG1871	478	I mind how once we lay, such a transparent summer morning;
LG1871	479	How you settled your head athwart my hips, and gently turn'd over upon me,
LG1871	480	And parted my shirt from my bosom-bone, and plunged your tongue to my bare-stript heart,
LG1871	481	And reach'd till you felt my beard, and reach'd till you held my feet.
LG1871	482	Swiftly arose and spread around me the peace and knowledge that pass all the argument of the earth;
LG1871	483	And I know that the hand of God is the promise of my own,
LG1871	484	And I know that the spirit of God is the brother of my own;
LG1871	485	And that all the men ever born are also my brothers, and the women my sisters and lovers;
LG1871	486	And that a kelson of the creation is love;
LG1871	487	And limitless are leaves, stiff or drooping in the fields;
LG1871	488	And brown ants in the little wells beneath them;
LG1871	489	And mossy scabs of the worm fence, and heap'd stones, elder, mullen, and poke-weed.
LG1871	490	A child said, What is the grass? fetching it to me with full hands;
LG1871	491	How could I answer the child? I do not know what it is, any more than he.
LG1871	492	I guess it must be the flag of my disposition, out of hopeful green stuff woven.
LG1871	493	Or I guess it is the handkerchief of the Lord,
LG1871	494	A scented gift and remembrancer, designedly dropt,
LG1871	495	Bearing the owner's name someway in the corners, that we may see and remark, and say, Whose?
LG1871	496	Or I guess the grass is itself a child, the produced babe of the vegetation.
LG1871	497	Or I guess it is a uniform hieroglyphic;
LG1871	498	And it means, Sprouting alike in broad zones and nar-rownarrow zones,
LG1871	499	Growing among black folks as among white;
LG1871	500	Kanuck, Tuckahoe, Congressman, Cuff, I give them the same, I receive them the same.
LG1871	501	And now it seems to me the beautiful uncut hair of graves.
LG1871	502	Tenderly will I use you, curling grass;
LG1871	503	It may be you transpire from the breasts of young men;
LG1871	504	It may be if I had known them I would have loved them;
LG1871	505	It may be you are from old people and from women, and from offspring taken soon out of their mothers' laps;
LG1871	506	And here you are the mothers' laps;
LG1871	507	This grass is very dark to be from the white heads of old mothers;
LG1871	508	Darker than the colourless beards of old men;
LG1871	509	Dark to come from under the faint red roofs of mouths.
LG1871	510	O I perceive after all so many uttering tongues!
LG1871	511	And I perceive they do not come from the roofs of mouths for nothing.
LG1871	512	I wish I could translate the hints about the dead young men and women,
LG1871	513	And the hints about old men and mothers, and the offspring taken soon out of their laps.
LG1871	514	What do you think has become of the young and old men?
LG1871	515	And what do you think has become of the women and children?
LG1871	516	They are alive and well somewhere;
LG1871	517	The smallest sprout shows there is really no death;
LG1871	518	And if ever there was, it led forward life, and does not wait at the end to arrest it,
LG1871	519	And ceas'd the moment life appear'd.
LG1871	520	All goes onward and outward—nothing collapses;
LG1871	521	And to die is different from what any one supposed, and luckier.
LG1871	522	Has any one supposed it lucky to be born?
LG1871	523	I hasten to inform him or her, it is just as lucky to die, and I know it.
LG1871	524	I pass death with the dying, and birth with the new-wash'dnew-wash'd babe, and am not contain'd between my hat and boots;
LG1871	525	And peruse manifold objects, no two alike, and every one good;
LG1871	526	The earth good, and the stars good, and their adjuncts all good.
LG1871	527	I am not on earth, nor an adjunct of an earth;
LG1871	528	I am the mate and companion of people, all just as immortal and fathomless as myself;
LG1871	529	(They do not know how immortal, but I know.)
LG1871	530	Every kind for itself and its own—for me mine, male and female;
LG1871	531	For me those that have been boys, and that love women;
LG1871	532	For me the man that is proud, and feels how it stings to be slighted;
LG1871	533	For me the sweet-heart and the old maid—for me mothers, and the mothers of mothers;
LG1871	534	For me lips that have smiled, eyes that have shed tears;
LG1871	535	For me children, and the begetters of children.
LG1871	536	Undrape! you are not guilty to me, nor stale, nor discarded;
LG1871	537	I see through the broadcloth and gingham, whether or no;
LG1871	538	And am around, tenacious, acquisitive, tireless, and cannot be shaken away.
LG1871	539	The little one sleeps in its cradle;
LG1871	540	I lift the gauze, and look a long time, and silently brush away flies with my hand.
LG1871	541	The youngster and the red-faced girl turn aside up the bushy hill;
LG1871	542	I peeringly view them from the top.
LG1871	543	The suicide sprawls on the bloody floor of the bed-roombedroom;
LG1871	544	I witness the corpse with its dabbled hair—I note where the pistol has fallen.
LG1871	545	The blab of the pave, the tires of carts, sluff of boot-solesboot-soles, talk of the promenaders;
LG1871	546	The heavy omnibus, the driver with his interrogating thumb, the clank of the shod horses on the granite floor;
LG1871	547	The snow-sleighs, the clinking, shouted jokes, pelts of snow-balls;
LG1871	548	The hurrahs for popular favorites, the fury of rous'd mobs;
LG1871	549	The flap of the curtain'd litter, a sick man inside, borne to the hospital;
LG1871	550	The meeting of enemies, the sudden oath, the blows and fall;
LG1871	551	The excited crowd, the policeman with his star, quickly working his passage to the centre of the crowd;
LG1871	552	The impassive stones that receive and return so many echoes;
LG1871	553	What groans of over-fed or half-starv'd who fall sun-strucksunstruck, or in fits;
LG1871	554	What exclamations of women taken suddenly, who hurry home and give birth to babes;
LG1871	555	What living and buried speech is always vibrating here—what howls restrain'd by decorum;
LG1871	556	Arrests of criminals, slights, adulterous offers made, acceptances, rejections with convex lips;
LG1871	557	I mind them or the show or resonance of them—I come, and I depart.
LG1871	558	The big doors of the country barn stand open and ready;
LG1871	559	The dried grass of the harvest-time loads the slow-drawnslow-drawn wagon;
LG1871	560	The clear light plays on the brown gray and green intertinged;
LG1871	561	The armfuls are pack'd to the sagging mow.
LG1871	562	I am there—I help—I came stretch'd atop of the load;
LG1871	563	I felt its soft jolts—one leg reclined on the other;
LG1871	564	I jump from the cross-beams, and seize the clover and timothy,
LG1871	565	And roll head over heels, and tangle my hair full of wisps.
LG1871	566	Alone, far in the wilds and mountains, I hunt,
LG1871	567	Wandering, amazed at my own lightness and glee;
LG1871	568	In the late afternoon choosing a safe spot to pass the night,
LG1871	569	Kindling a fire and broiling the fresh-kill'd game;
LG1871	570	Falling asleep on the gather'd leaves, with my dog and gun by my side.
LG1871	571	The Yankee clipper is under her sky-sails—she cuts the sparkle and scud;
LG1871	572	My eyes settle the land—I bend at her prow, or shout joyously from the deck.
LG1871	573	The boatmen and clam-diggers arose early and stopt for me;
LG1871	574	I tuck'd my trowser-ends in my boots, and went and had a good time:
LG1871	575	(You should have been with us that day round the chowder-kettle.)
LG1871	576	I saw the marriage of the trapper in the open air in the far west—the bride was a red girl;
LG1871	577	Her father and his friends sat near, cross-legged and dumbly smoking—they had moccasins to their feet, and large thick blankets hanging from their shoulders;
LG1871	578	On a bank lounged the trapper—he was drest mostly in skins—his luxuriant beard and curls protected his neck—he held his bride by the hand;
LG1871	579	She had long eyelashes—her head was bare—her coarse straight locks descended upon her voluptuous limbs and reach'd to her feet.
LG1871	580	The runaway slave came to my house and stopt out-sideoutside;
LG1871	581	I heard his motions crackling the twigs of the wood-pilewoodpile;
LG1871	582	Through the swung half-door of the kitchen I saw him limpsy and weak,
LG1871	583	And went where he sat on a log, and led him in and assured him,
LG1871	584	And brought water, and fill'd a tub for his sweated body and bruis'd feet,
LG1871	585	And gave him a room that enter'd from my own, and gave him some coarse clean clothes,
LG1871	586	And remember perfectly well his revolving eyes and his awkwardness,
LG1871	587	And remember putting plasters on the galls of his neck and ankles;
LG1871	588	He staid with me a week before he was recuperated and pass'd north;
LG1871	589	(I had him sit next me at table—my fire-lock lean'd in the corner.)
LG1871	590	Twenty-eight young men bathe by the shore;
LG1871	591	Twenty-eight young men, and all so friendly:
LG1871	592	Twenty-eight years of womanly life, and all so lone-somelonesome.
LG1871	593	She owns the fine house by the rise of the bank;
LG1871	594	She hides, handsome and richly drest, aft the blinds of the window.
LG1871	595	Which of the young men does she like the best?
LG1871	596	Ah, the homeliest of them is beautiful to her.
LG1871	597	Where are you off to, lady? for I see you;
LG1871	598	You splash in the water there, yet stay stock still in your room.
LG1871	599	Dancing and laughing along the beach came the twenty-ninth bather;
LG1871	600	The rest did not see her, but she saw them and loved them.
LG1871	601	The beards of the young men glisten'd with wet, it ran from their long hair:
LG1871	602	Little streams pass'd all over their bodies.
LG1871	603	An unseen hand also pass'd over their bodies;
LG1871	604	It descended tremblingly from their temples and ribs.
LG1871	605	The young men float on their backs—their white bel-liesbellies bulge to the sun—they do not ask who seizes fast to them;
LG1871	606	They do not know who puffs and declines with pendant and bending arch;
LG1871	607	They do not think whom they souse with spray.
LG1871	608	The butcher-boy puts off his killing clothes, or sharpens his knife at the stall in the market;
LG1871	609	I loiter, enjoying his repartee, and his shuffle and break-down.
LG1871	610	Blacksmiths with grimed and hairy chests environ the anvil;
LG1871	611	Each has his main-sledge—they are all out—(there is a great heat in the fire.)
LG1871	612	From the cinder-strew'd threshold I follow their movements;
LG1871	613	The lithe sheer of their waists plays even with their massive arms;
LG1871	614	Over-hand the hammers swing—over-hand so slow— over-hand so sure:
LG1871	615	They do not hasten—each man hits in his place.
LG1871	616	The negro holds firmly the reins of his four horses —the block swags underneath on its tied-over chain;
LG1871	617	The negro that drives the dray of the stone-yard— steady and tall he stands, pois'd on one leg on the string-piece;
LG1871	618	His blue shirt exposes his ample neck and breast, and loosens over his hip-band;
LG1871	619	His glance is calm and commanding—he tosses the slouch of his hat away from his forehead;
LG1871	620	The sun falls on his crispy hair and moustache—falls on the black of his polish'd and perfect limbs.
LG1871	621	I behold the picturesque giant, and love him—and I do not stop there;
LG1871	622	I go with the team also.
LG1871	623	In me the caresser of life wherever moving—back-wardbackward as well as forward slueing;
LG1871	624	To niches aside and junior bending.
LG1871	625	Oxen that rattle the yoke and chain, or halt in the leafy shade! what is that you express in your eyes?
LG1871	626	It seems to me more than all the print I have read in my life.
LG1871	627	My tread scares the wood-drake and wood-duck, on my distant and day-long ramble;
LG1871	628	They rise together—they slowly circle around.
LG1871	629	I believe in those wing'd purposes,
LG1871	630	And acknowledge red, yellow, white, playing within me,
LG1871	631	And consider green and violet, and the tufted crown, intentional;
LG1871	632	And do not call the tortoise unworthy because she is not something else;
LG1871	633	And the jay in the woods never studied the gamut, yet trills pretty well to me;
LG1871	634	And the look of the bay mare shames silliness out of me.
LG1871	635	The wild gander leads his flock through the cool night;
LG1871	636	Ya-honk! he says, and sounds it down to me like an invitation;
LG1871	637	(The pert may suppose it meaningless, but I listen close;
LG1871	638	I find its purpose and place up there toward the wintry sky.)
LG1871	639	The sharp-hoof'd moose of the north, the cat on the house-sill, the chickadee, the prairie-dog,
LG1871	640	The litter of the grunting sow as they tug at her teats,
LG1871	641	The brood of the turkey-hen, and she with her half-spreadhalf-spread wings;
LG1871	642	I see in them and myself the same old law.
LG1871	643	The press of my foot to the earth springs a hundred affections;
LG1871	644	They scorn the best I can do to relate them.
LG1871	645	I am enamour'd of growing out-doors,
LG1871	646	Of men that live among cattle, or taste of the ocean or woods,
LG1871	647	Of the builders and steerers of ships, and the wielders of axes and mauls, and the drivers of horses;
LG1871	648	I can eat and sleep with them week in and week out.
LG1871	649	What is commonest, cheapest, nearest, easiest, is Me;
LG1871	650	Me going in for my chances, spending for vast returns;
LG1871	651	Adorning myself to bestow myself on the first that will take me;
LG1871	652	Not asking the sky to come down to my good will;
LG1871	653	Scattering it freely forever.
LG1871	654	The pure contralto sings in the organ loft;
LG1871	655	The carpenter dresses his plank—the tongue of his foreplane whistles its wild ascending lisp;
LG1871	656	The married and unmarried children ride home to their Thanksgiving dinner;
LG1871	657	The pilot seizes the king-pin—he heaves down with a strong arm;
LG1871	658	The mate stands braced in the whale-boat—lance and harpoon are ready;
LG1871	659	The duck-shooter walks by silent and cautious stretches;
LG1871	660	The deacons are ordain'd with cross'd hands at the altar;
LG1871	661	The spinning-girl retreats and advances to the hum of the big wheel;
LG1871	662	The farmer stops by the bars, as he walks on a First- day loafe, and looks at the oats and rye;
LG1871	663	The lunatic is carried at last to the asylum, a confirm'd case,
LG1871	664	(He will never sleep any more as he did in the cot in his mother's bedroom;)
LG1871	665	The jour printer with gray head and gaunt jaws works at his case,
LG1871	666	He turns his quid of tobacco, while his eyes blurr with the manuscript;
LG1871	667	The malform'd limbs are tied to the surgeon's table,
LG1871	668	What is removed drops horribly in a pail;
LG1871	669	The quadroon girl is sold at the auction-stand—the drunkard nods by the bar-room stove;
LG1871	670	The machinist rolls up his sleeves—the policeman trav-elstravels his beat—the gate-keeper marks who pass;
LG1871	671	The young fellow drives the express-wagon—(I love him, though I do not know him;)
LG1871	672	The half-breed straps on his light boots to compete in the race;
LG1871	673	The western turkey-shooting draws old and young— some lean on their rifles, some sit on logs,
LG1871	674	Out from the crowd steps the marksman, takes his position, levels his piece;
LG1871	675	The groups of newly-come immigrants cover the wharf or levee;
LG1871	676	As the woolly-pates hoe in the sugar-field, the overseer views them from his saddle;
LG1871	677	The bugle calls in the ball-room, the gentlemen run for their partners, the dancers bow to each other;
LG1871	678	The youth lies awake in the cedar-roof'd garret, and harks to the musical rain;
LG1871	679	The Wolverine sets traps on the creek that helps fill the Huron;
LG1871	680	The squaw, wrapt in her yellow-hemm'd cloth, is offer-ingoffering moccasins and bead-bags for sale;
LG1871	681	The connoisseur peers along the exhibition-gallery with half-shut eyes bent sideways;
LG1871	682	As the deck-hands make fast the steamboat, the plank is thrown for the shore-going passengers;
LG1871	683	The young sister holds out the skein, while the elder sister winds it off in a ball, and stops now and then for the knots;
LG1871	684	The one-year wife is recovering and happy, having a week ago borne her first child;
LG1871	685	The clean-hair'd Yankee girl works with her sewing-machinesewing-machine, or in the factory or mill;
LG1871	686	The nine months' gone is in the parturition chamber, her faintness and pains are advancing;
LG1871	687	The paving-man leans on his two-handed rammer—the reporter's lead flies swiftly over the note-book— the sign-painter is lettering with red and gold;
LG1871	688	The canal-boy trots on the tow-path—the book-keeper counts at his desk—the shoemaker waxes his thread;
LG1871	689	The conductor beats time for the band, and all the per-formersperformers follow him;
LG1871	690	The child is baptized—the convert is making his first professions;
LG1871	691	The regatta is spread on the bay—the race is begun— how the white sails sparkle!
LG1871	692	The drover, watching his drove, sings out to them that would stray;
LG1871	693	The pedler sweats with his pack on his back, (the pur-chaserpurchaser higgling about the odd cent;)
LG1871	694	The camera and plate are prepared, the lady must sit for her daguerreotype;
LG1871	695	The bride unrumples her white dress, the minute-hand of the clock moves slowly;
LG1871	696	The opium-eater reclines with rigid head and just- open'd lips;
LG1871	697	The prostitute draggles her shawl, her bonnet bobs on her tipsy and pimpled neck;
LG1871	698	The crowd laugh at her blackguard oaths, the men jeer and wink to each other;
LG1871	699	(Miserable! I do not laugh at your oaths, nor jeer you;)
LG1871	700	The President, holding a cabinet council, is surrounded by the Great Secretaries;
LG1871	701	On the piazza walk three matrons stately and friendly with twined arms;
LG1871	702	The crew of the fish-smack pack repeated layers of hal-ibuthalibut in the hold;
LG1871	703	The Missourian crosses the plains, toting his wares and his cattle;
LG1871	704	As the fare-collector goes through the train, he gives notice by the jingling of loose change;
LG1871	705	The floor-men are laying the floor—the tinners are tinning the roof—the masons are calling for mortar;
LG1871	706	In single file, each shouldering his hod, pass onward the laborers;
LG1871	707	Seasons pursuing each other, the indescribable crowd is gather'd—it is the Fourth of Seventh-month— (What salutes of cannon and small arms!)
LG1871	708	Seasons pursuing each other, the plougher ploughs, the mower mows, and the winter-grain falls in the ground;
LG1871	709	Off on the lakes the pike-fisher watches and waits by the hole in the frozen surface;
LG1871	710	The stumps stand thick round the clearing, the squatter strikes deep with his axe;
LG1871	711	Flatboatmen make fast, towards dusk, near the cotton-woodcottonwood or pekan-trees;
LG1871	712	Coon-seekers go through the regions of the Red river, or through those drain'd by the Tennessee, or through those of the Arkansaw;
LG1871	713	Torches shine in the dark that hangs on the Chatta-hoocheChattahooche or Altamahaw;
LG1871	714	Patriarchs sit at supper with sons and grandsons and great-grandsons around them;
LG1871	715	In walls of adobie, in canvas tents, rest hunters and trappers after their day's sport;
LG1871	716	The city sleeps, and the country sleeps;
LG1871	717	The living sleep for their time, the dead sleep for their time;
LG1871	718	The old husband sleeps by his wife, and the young hus-bandhusband sleeps by his wife;
LG1871	719	And these one and all tend inward to me, and I tend outward to them;
LG1871	720	And such as it is to be of these, more or less, I am.
LG1871	721	I am of old and young, of the foolish as much as the wise;
LG1871	722	Regardless of others, ever regardful of others,
LG1871	723	Maternal as well as paternal, a child as well as a man,
LG1871	724	Stuff'd with the stuff that is coarse, and stuff'd with the stuff that is fine;
LG1871	725	One of the Great Nation, the nation of many nations, the smallest the same, and the largest the same;
LG1871	726	A southerner soon as a northerner—a planter non-chalantnonchalant and hospitable, down by the Oconee I live;
LG1871	727	A Yankee, bound my own way, ready for trade, my joints the limberest joints on earth, and the stern-eststernest joints on earth;
LG1871	728	A Kentuckian, walking the vale of the Elkhorn, in my deer-skin leggings—a Louisianian or Georgian;
LG1871	729	A boatman over lakes or bays, or along coasts—a Hoosier, Badger, Buckeye;
LG1871	730	At home on Kanadian snow-shoes, or up in the bush, or with fishermen off Newfoundland;
LG1871	731	At home in the fleet of ice-boats, sailing with the rest and tacking;
LG1871	732	At home on the hills of Vermont; or in the woods of Maine, or the Texan ranch;
LG1871	733	Comrade of Californians—comrade of free north-west-ernersnorth-westerners, (loving their big proportions;)
LG1871	734	Comrade of raftsmen and coalmen—comrade of all who shake hands and welcome to drink and meat;
LG1871	735	A learner with the simplest, a teacher of the thought-fullestthoughtfullest;
LG1871	736	A novice beginning, yet experient of myriads of sea-sonsseasons;
LG1871	737	Of every hue and caste am I, of every rank and reli-gionreligion;
LG1871	738	A farmer, mechanic, artist, gentleman, sailor, quaker;
LG1871	739	A prisoner, fancy-man, rowdy, lawyer, physician, priest.
LG1871	740	I resist anything better than my own diversity;
LG1871	741	I breathe the air, but leave plenty after me,
LG1871	742	And am not stuck up, and am in my place.
LG1871	743	(The moth and the fish-eggs are in their place;
LG1871	744	The suns I see, and the suns I cannot see, are in their place;
LG1871	745	The, palpable is in its place, and the impalpable is in its place.)
LG1871	746	These are the thoughts of all men in all ages and lands—they are not original with me;
LG1871	747	If they are not yours as much as mine, they are nothing, or next to nothing;
LG1871	748	If they are not the riddle, and the untying of the riddle, they are nothing;
LG1871	749	If they are not just as close as they are distant, they are nothing.
LG1871	750	This is the grass that grows wherever the land is, and the water is;
LG1871	751	This is the common air that bathes the globe.
LG1871	752	With music strong I come—with my cornets and my drums,
LG1871	753	I play not marches for accepted victors only—I play great marches for conquered and slain persons.
LG1871	754	Have you heard that it was good to gain the day?
LG1871	755	I also say it is good to fall—battles are lost in the same spirit in which they are won.
LG1871	756	I beat and pound for the dead;
LG1871	757	I blow through my embouchures my loudest and gayest for them.
LG1871	758	Vivas to those who have fail'd!
LG1871	759	And to those whose war-vessels sank in the sea!
LG1871	760	And to those themselves who sank in the sea!
LG1871	761	And to all generals that lost engagements! and all over-comeovercome heroes!
LG1871	762	And the numberless unknown heroes, equal to the greatest heroes known.
LG1871	763	This is the meal equally set—this is the meat for natural hunger;
LG1871	764	It is for the wicked just the same as the righteous—I make appointments with all;
LG1871	765	I will not have a single person slighted or left away;
LG1871	766	The kept-woman, sponger, thief, are hereby invited;
LG1871	767	The heavy-lipp'd slave is invited—the venerealee is in-vitedinvited:
LG1871	768	There shall be no difference between them and the rest.
LG1871	769	This is the press of a bashful hand—this is the float and odor of hair;
LG1871	770	This is the touch of my lips to yours—this is the mur-murmurmur of yearning:
LG1871	771	This is the far-off depth and height reflecting my own face;
LG1871	772	This is the thoughtful merge of myself, and the outlet again.
LG1871	773	Do you guess I have some intricate purpose?
LG1871	774	Well, I have—for the Fourth-month showers have, and the mica on the side of a rock has.
LG1871	775	Do you take it I would astonish?
LG1871	776	Does the daylight astonish? Does the early redstart, twittering through the woods?
LG1871	777	Do I astonish more than they?
LG1871	778	This hour I tell things in confidence;
LG1871	779	I might not tell everybody, but I will tell you.
LG1871	780	Who goes there? hankering, gross, mystical, nude;
LG1871	781	How is it I extract strength from the beef I eat?
LG1871	782	What is a man, anyhow? What am I? What are you?
LG1871	783	All I mark as my own, you shall offset it with your own;
LG1871	784	Else it were time lost listening to me.
LG1871	785	I do not snivel that snivel the world over,
LG1871	786	That months are vacuums, and the ground but wallow and filth;
LG1871	787	That life is a suck and a sell, and nothing remains at the end but threadbare crape, and tears.
LG1871	788	Whimpering and truckling fold with powders for invalids—conformity goes to the fourth-remov'd;
LG1871	789	I wear my hat as I please, indoors or out.
LG1871	790	Why should I pray? Why should I venerate and be ceremonious?
LG1871	791	Having pried through the strata, analyzed to a hair, counsell'd with doctors, and calculated close,
LG1871	792	I find no sweeter fat than sticks to my own bones.
LG1871	793	In all people I see myself—none more, and not one a barley-corn less;
LG1871	794	And the good or bad I say of myself, I say of them.
LG1871	795	And I know I am solid and sound;
LG1871	796	To me the converging objects of the universe perpetu-allyperpetually flow;
LG1871	797	All are written to me, and I must get what the writing means.
LG1871	798	I know I am deathless;
LG1871	799	I know this orbit of mine cannot be swept by the car-penter'scarpenter's compass;
LG1871	800	I know I shall not pass like a child's carlacue cut with a burnt stick at night.
LG1871	801	I know I am august;
LG1871	802	I do not trouble my spirit to vindicate itself or be understood;
LG1871	803	I see that the elementary laws never apologize;
LG1871	804	(I reckon I behave no prouder than the level I plant my house by, after all.)
LG1871	805	I exist as I am—that is enough;
LG1871	806	If no other in the world be aware, I sit content;
LG1871	807	And if each and all be aware, I sit content;
LG1871	808	One world is aware, and by far the largest to me, and that is myself;
LG1871	809	And whether I come to my own to-day, or in ten thousand or ten million years,
LG1871	810	I can cheerfully take it now, or with equal cheerfulness I can wait.
LG1871	811	My foothold is tenon'd and mortis'd in granite;
LG1871	812	I laugh at what you call dissolution;
LG1871	813	And I know the amplitude of time.
LG1871	814	I am the poet of the Body;
LG1871	815	And I am the poet of the Soul.
LG1871	816	The pleasures of heaven are with me, and the pains of hell are with me;
LG1871	817	The first I graft and increase upon myself—the latter I translate into a new tongue.
LG1871	818	I am the poet of the woman the same as the man;
LG1871	819	And I say it is as great to be a woman as to be a man;
LG1871	820	And I say there is nothing greater than the mother of men.
LG1871	821	I chant the chant of dilation or pride;
LG1871	822	We have had ducking and deprecating about enough;
LG1871	823	I show that size is only development.
LG1871	824	Have you outstript the rest? Are you the Presi-dentPresident?
LG1871	825	It is a trifle—they will more than arrive there, every one, and still pass on.
LG1871	826	I am he that walks with the tender and growing night;
LG1871	827	I call to the earth and sea, half-held by the night.
LG1871	828	Press close, bare-bosom'd night! Press close, mag-neticmagnetic, nourishing night!
LG1871	829	Night of south winds! night of the large few stars!
LG1871	830	Still, nodding night! mad, naked, summer night.
LG1871	831	Smile, O voluptuous, cool-breath'd earth!
LG1871	832	Earth of the slumbering and liquid trees;
LG1871	833	Earth of departed sunset! earth of the mountains, misty-topt!
LG1871	834	Earth of the vitreous pour of the full moon, just tinged with blue!
LG1871	835	Earth of shine and dark, mottling the tide of the river!
LG1871	836	Earth of the limpid gray of clouds, brighter and clearer for my sake!
LG1871	837	Far-swooping elbow'd earth! rich, apple-blossom'd earth!
LG1871	838	Smile, for your lover comes!
LG1871	839	Prodigal, you have given me love! Therefore I to you give love!
LG1871	840	O unspeakable, passionate love!
LG1871	841	You sea! I resign myself to you also—I guess what you mean;
LG1871	842	I behold from the beach your crooked inviting fingers;
LG1871	843	I believe you refuse to go back without feeling of me;
LG1871	844	We must have a turn together—I undress—hurry me out of sight of the land;
LG1871	845	Cushion me soft, rock me in billowy drowse;
LG1871	846	Dash me with amorous wet—I can repay you.
LG1871	847	Sea of stretch'd ground-swells!
LG1871	848	Sea breathing broad and convulsive breaths!
LG1871	849	Sea of the brine of life! sea of unshovell'd yet always-readyalways-ready graves!
LG1871	850	Howler and scooper of storms! capricious and dainty sea!
LG1871	851	I am integral with you—I too am of one phase, and of all phases.
LG1871	852	Partaker of influx and efflux I—extoller of hate and conciliation;
LG1871	853	Extoller of amies, and those that sleep in each others' arms.
LG1871	854	I am he attesting sympathy;
LG1871	855	(Shall I make my list of things in the house, and skip the house that supports them?)
LG1871	856	I am not the poet of goodness only—I do not decline to be the poet of wickedness also.
LG1871	857	Washes and razors for foofoos—for me freckles and a bristling beard.
LG1871	858	What blurt is this about virtue and about vice?
LG1871	859	Evil propels me, and reform of evil propels me—I stand indifferent;
LG1871	860	My gait is no fault-finder's or rejecter's gait;
LG1871	861	I moisten the roots of all that has grown.
LG1871	862	Did you fear some scrofula out of the unflagging pregnancy?
LG1871	863	Did you guess the celestial laws are yet to be work'd over and rectified?
LG1871	864	I find one side a balance, and the antipodal side a balance;
LG1871	865	Soft doctrine as steady help as stable doctrine;
LG1871	866	Thoughts and deeds of the present, our rouse and early start.
LG1871	867	This minute that comes to me over the past decil-lionsdecillions,
LG1871	868	There is no better than it and now.
LG1871	869	What behaved well in the past, or behaves well to-dayto-day, is not such a wonder;
LG1871	870	The wonder is, always and always, how there can be a mean man or an infidel.
LG1871	871	Endless unfolding of words of ages!
LG1871	872	And mine a word of the modern—the word En-Masse.
LG1871	873	A word of the faith that never balks;
LG1871	874	Here or henceforward, it is all the same to me—I accept Time, absolutely.
LG1871	875	It alone is without flaw—it rounds and completes all;
LG1871	876	That mystic, baffling wonder I love, alone completes all.
LG1871	877	I accept reality, and dare not question it;
LG1871	878	Materialism first and last imbuing.
LG1871	879	Hurrah for positive science! long live exact demon-strationdemonstration!
LG1871	880	Fetch stonecrop, mixt with cedar and branches of lilac;
LG1871	881	This is the lexicographer—this the chemist—this made a grammar of the old cartouches;
LG1871	882	These mariners put the ship through dangerous un-knownunknown seas;
LG1871	883	This is the geologist—this works with the scalpel—and this is a mathematician.
LG1871	884	Gentlemen! to you the first honors always:
LG1871	885	Your facts are useful and real—and yet they are not my dwelling;
LG1871	886	(I but enter by them to an area of my dwelling.)
LG1871	887	Less the reminders of properties told, my words;
LG1871	888	And more the reminders, they, of life untold, and of freedom and extrication,
LG1871	889	And make short account of neuters and geldings, and favor men and women fully equipt,
LG1871	890	And beat the gong of revolt, and stop with fugitives, and them that plot and conspire.
LG1871	891	Walt Whitman am I, a Kosmos, of mighty Manhat-tanManhattan the son,
LG1871	892	Turbulent, fleshy and sensual, eating, drinking and breeding;
LG1871	893	No sentimentalist—no stander above men and women, or apart from them;
LG1871	894	No more modest than immodest.
LG1871	895	Unscrew the locks from the doors!
LG1871	896	Unscrew the doors themselves from their jambs!
LG1871	897	Whoever degrades another degrades me;
LG1871	898	And whatever is done or said returns at last to me.
LG1871	899	Through me the afflatus surging and surging— through me the current and index.
LG1871	900	I speak the pass-word primeval—I give the sign of democracy;
LG1871	901	By God! I will accept nothing which all cannot have their counterpart of on the same terms.
LG1871	902	Through me many long dumb voices;
LG1871	903	Voices of the interminable generations of slaves;
LG1871	904	Voices of prostitutes, and of deform'd persons;
LG1871	905	Voices of the diseas'd and despairing, and of thieves and dwarfs;
LG1871	906	Voices of cycles of preparation and accretion,
LG1871	907	And of the threads that connect the stars—and of wombs and of the father-stuff,
LG1871	908	And of the rights of them the others are down upon;
LG1871	909	Of the trivial, flat, foolish, despised,
LG1871	910	Fog in the air, beetles rolling balls of dung.
LG1871	911	Through me forbidden voices;
LG1871	912	Voices of sexes and lusts—voices veil'd, and I remove the veil;
LG1871	913	Voices indecent, by me clarified and transfigur'd.
LG1871	914	I do not press my fingers across my mouth;
LG1871	915	I keep as delicate around the bowels as around the head and heart;
LG1871	916	Copulation is no more rank to me than death is.
LG1871	917	I believe in the flesh and the appetites;
LG1871	918	Seeing, hearing, feeling, are miracles, and each part and tag of me is a miracle.
LG1871	919	Divine am I inside and out, and I make holy what-everwhatever I touch or am touch'd from;
LG1871	920	The scent of these arm-pits, aroma finer than prayer;
LG1871	921	This head more than churches, bibles, and all the creeds.
LG1871	922	If I worship one thing more than another, it shall be the spread of my own body, or any part of it.
LG1871	923	Translucent mould of me, it shall be you!
LG1871	924	Shaded ledges and rests, it shall be you!
LG1871	925	Firm masculine colter, it shall be you.
LG1871	926	Whatever goes to the tilth of me, it shall be you!
LG1871	927	You my rich blood! Your milky stream, pale strippings of my life.
LG1871	928	Breast that presses against other breasts, it shall be you!
LG1871	929	My brain, it shall be your occult convolutions.
LG1871	930	Root of wash'd sweet flag! timorous pond-snipe! nest of guarded duplicate eggs! it shall be you!
LG1871	931	Mix'd tussled hay of head, beard, brawn, it shall be you!
LG1871	932	Trickling sap of maple! fibre of manly wheat! it shall be you!
LG1871	933	Sun so generous, it shall be you!
LG1871	934	Vapors lighting and shading my face, it shall be you!
LG1871	935	You sweaty brooks and dews, it shall be you!
LG1871	936	Winds whose soft-tickling genitals rub against me, it shall be you!
LG1871	937	Broad, muscular fields! branches of live oak! loving lounger in my winding paths! it shall be you!
LG1871	938	Hands I have taken—face I have kiss'd—mortal I have ever touch'd! it shall be you.
LG1871	939	I dote on myself—there is that lot of me, and all so luscious;
LG1871	940	Each moment, and whatever happens, thrills me with joy.
LG1871	941	O I am wonderful!
LG1871	942	I cannot tell how my ankles bend, nor whence the cause of my faintest wish;
LG1871	943	Nor the cause of the friendship I emit, nor the cause of the friendship I take again.
LG1871	944	That I walk up my stoop! I pause to consider if it really be;
LG1871	945	A morning-glory at my window satisfies me more than the metaphysics of books.
LG1871	946	To behold the day-break!
LG1871	947	The little light fades the immense and diaphanous shadows;
LG1871	948	The air tastes good to my palate.
LG1871	949	Hefts of the moving world, at innocent gambols, silently rising, freshly exuding,
LG1871	950	Scooting obliquely high and low.
LG1871	951	Something I cannot see puts upward libidinous prongs;
LG1871	952	Seas of bright juice suffuse heaven.
LG1871	953	The earth by the sky staid with—the daily close of their junction;
LG1871	954	The heav'd challenge from the east that moment over my head;
LG1871	955	The mocking taunt, See then whether you shall be master!
LG1871	956	Dazzling and tremendous, how quick the sun-rise would kill me,
LG1871	957	If I could not now and always send sun-rise out of me.
LG1871	958	We also ascend, dazzling and tremendous as the sun;
LG1871	959	We found our own, O my Soul, in the calm and cool of the daybreak.
LG1871	960	My voice goes after what my eyes cannot reach;
LG1871	961	With the twirl of my tongue I encompass worlds, and volumes of worlds.
LG1871	962	Speech is the twin of my vision—it is unequal to measure itself;
LG1871	963	It provokes me forever;
LG1871	964	It says sarcastically, Walt, you contain enough—why don't you let it out, then?
LG1871	965	Come now, I will not be tantalized—you conceive too much of articulation.
LG1871	966	Do you not know, O speech, how the buds beneath you are folded?
LG1871	967	Waiting in gloom, protected by frost;
LG1871	968	The dirt receding before my prophetical screams;
LG1871	969	I underlying causes, to balance them at last;
LG1871	970	My knowledge my live parts—it keeping tally with the meaning of things,
LG1871	971	HAPPINESS—which, whoever hears me, let him or her set out in search of this day.
LG1871	972	My final merit I refuse you—I refuse putting from me what I really am;
LG1871	973	Encompass worlds, but never try to encompass me;
LG1871	974	I crowd your sleekest and best by simply looking to-wardtoward you.
LG1871	975	Writing and talk do not prove me;
LG1871	976	I carry the plenum of proof, and everything else, in my face;
LG1871	977	With the hush of my lips I wholly confound the skep-ticskeptic.
LG1871	978	I think I will do nothing now but listen,
LG1871	979	To accrue what I hear into myself—to let sounds con-tributecontribute toward me.
LG1871	980	I hear bravuras of birds, bustle of growing wheat, gossip of flames, clack of sticks cooking my meals;
LG1871	981	I hear the sound I love, the sound of the human voice;
LG1871	982	I hear all sounds running together, combined, fused or following;
LG1871	983	Sounds of the city, and sounds out of the city—sounds of the day and night;
LG1871	984	Talkative young ones to those that like them—the loud laugh of work-people at their meals;
LG1871	985	The angry base of disjointed friendship—the faint tones of the sick;
LG1871	986	The judge with hands tight to the desk, his pallid lips pronouncing a death-sentence;
LG1871	987	The heave'e'yo of stevedores unlading ships by the wharves—the refrain of the anchor-lifters;
LG1871	988	The ring of alarm-bells—the cry of fire—the whirr of swift-streaking engines and hose-carts, with pre-monitorypremonitory tinkles, and color'd lights;
LG1871	989	The steam-whistle—the solid roll of the train of ap-proachingapproaching cars;
